Operator-visible behavior: when a guard fires HARD, the operator sees a boxed BLOCKED message with the rule cited + override path (typically {D_TAG}_ACK=1 {D_TAG}_ACK_REASON='<why>' <cmd>; honor-system at env-var level, audit-logged). When SOFT, operator sees an advisory but tool call proceeds. Full coverage table at holding/DIRECTION-ENFORCEMENT.md.

§1.6 How to read this document

  • Read §1 first to understand the operating model + Product Hierarchy.
  • §2 is the CoS Product PRD — operator-facing demands.
  • §3-§7 describe other layers; each starts with Purpose · Owns · Depends on · Does not own header.
  • Modules (Daily Pulse §7.5 · Feedback Engine §6.6) reference back to §1.4 for hierarchy semantics.
  • §8 Roadmap + §9 Meta close the document.
  • Every diagram is ASCII (text-readable by AI assistants and humans).
  • Per-pillar 3-ring structure (Surface · Contract · Mechanism) lives inside §2 B-pillars.

§2 Chief of Staff — Product (PRD) #

PurposeSpecify the first product on the Native platform — an implementation consultant for the operator (person in the company).
OwnsOperator's 5 demands (B1-B5); 24 needs; task taxonomy (T0-T3); actor taxonomy; per-pillar contracts; CX bar.
Depends on§4 Native platform primitives (Charters · Agents · Methods · Authority · Audit); §5 Connector transport.
Does NOT ownWorkforce deployment runtime; charter execution; tool transport; pricing; channels; observability instrumentation.

§2.A Idea — CoS product #

The WHY for CoS specifically. Problem · persona · anti-persona · vision · differentiation · scope · task taxonomy · actors. Operator-first; org is the mission frame, not the principal.

A.1 Problem statement #

Awaiting founder fill. Working frame: operator juggles N streams of work with sub-linear team scaling; existing assistants are conversational or chore-based; nothing compounds across the founder's actual work.

A.2 Persona #

Primary persona per Q1 ratified answer.

AttributeValue
RoleOperator running their company (single-company founder · multi-company founder · chief of staff · PM — see §A.2.1 for live examples)
SurfaceTerminal-fluent; design-particular
Time profileTime-poor; long-running cognitive context across days/weeks
Org contextFounder is principal; org is the mission frame, not the master

A.2.1 Concrete customer types (live examples)

Beyond the abstract persona, CoS v1 serves 4 concrete customer types — each with own workflow, decision cadence, and skill mode mix (see §A.9 Customer Skill Modes).

Customer typeExamplePrimary workflowDominant skill mode
Multi-company founderCEO of Testlify (B2B HRtech)Multi-company strategic decisions · board prep · vendor managementSM2 with SM1 for novel strategic asks
Chief of StaffCoS at Dharmik (D2C consumer)Operational orchestration · cross-functional comms · workshop facilitationSM3 with SM2 for novel asks
Product managerPM at a healthtech companyDiscovery + execution mix · stakeholder alignment · roadmap synthesisMix of SM1 (discovery) + SM2 (execution)
Co-founder + head-of-salesHead-of-sales co-founder at NeuricsSales pipeline · strategic deal-making · co-founder operational loadSM2 for sales workflows · SM1 for strategic decisions

A.2.2 Zero-terminal product constraint (post-onboarding · v1 + onward)

Hard rule (post-onboarding): after the one-time onboarding done by Asawa/Sutra Forge (per §6 Distribution), the operator never sees terminal commands, installation steps, or system internals during ongoing operation. Native is invisible during use. CoS surfaces only the operator's work (the 5 pillars). Any technical step (install · update · connect · authorize) is one-time setup done by Asawa/Sutra Forge or auto-onboarded via the Sutra plugin — not the operator. Operators don't type terminal commands. Operators don't read Sutra internals. CoS speaks the operator's domain language, not Native vocabulary. Scope clarification: v1 wedge requires terminal-fluent operator (per §A.6 + ratified Q2/Q9) — meaning operator CAN handle the one-time onboarding via terminal if needed; post-onboarding the operator works in their domain language only, never the terminal.

A.3 Anti-persona #

Operator types Native v1 explicitly does not serve.

#Anti-personaWhy not v1
AP1Casual chatbot userWorkflow-shaped, not conversational; one-off sessions don't compound
AP2Full-autonomy seekerOperator-in-loop is core; trust earned over time
AP3Non-terminal operatorv1 terminal-only per NG7 + Q9
AP4Single-tenant solo user (one company)Multi-tenant boundary management = the value
AP5Compliance-mandated black-box buyerv1 open-architecture + operator-managed

A.4 Vision #

Native is a workforce-deployment platform — it scopes, deploys, and supervises a workforce of agents under Charters, with Method routing, Authority gates, and a full audit trail.

Chief of Staff is the first product deployed on Native. CoS frames the operator's work, executes end-to-end where the path is known, surfaces decisions where operator judgment is needed, and closes the loop by writing what was decided + what shipped back into the substrate so the next iteration starts further ahead. CoS is an implementation consultant for the operator — accountable through the loop, not a day-organizer.

Every utterance + decision + outcome enters a compounding cognitive substrate. The operator's next ask is answered faster because the deployed CoS (running on Native) already knows the operator's patterns, charters, and history.

A.5 Differentiation #

Chief of Staff (this PRD) is NOTWhy the distinction matters
A day-organizer / meeting-prep aideCoS is an implementation consultant — takes work end-to-end to operationalized state, accountable through the loop
A chatbotWorkflow-shaped substrate; conversational is one surface, not the product
An autonomous agentOperator-in-loop on risky steps; trust earned, not assumed
A memory product (NG6)Governance + multi-tenant + emergence + closed-loop reuse — memory is downstream
A generic productivity toolSubstantive cognitive work for the operator; not calendar / todos

CoS is the first product on the Native platform (see Architecture context). Native itself is NOT a product — it's the workforce-deployment substrate. Distinctions above apply to the CoS product specifically.

A.6 Scope #

In-scope (v1)Out-of-scope (v2+ or NG)
Terminal-only operator surfaceGUI / web / mobile UI (NG1)
Single-LLM-provider abstractionMulti-LLM-provider abstraction (NG3)
Connector layer (Slack / email / web)Browser automation (NG5)
Metadata-based retrieval via 7aSemantic content-similarity retrieval (F1 v2+)
Mac + LinuxWindows v1
Multi-Human-Org tenancyPeer-Sutra context federation (v2+)
Person-Formation as opt-inRequired personalization
Native-Native A2A v1 stubFull A2A coordination protocol (v2+)
Operator-in-loop on risky stepsFull autonomy without operator gates

A.7 Task Taxonomy #

Native handles four task tiers, each with different time horizon, decomposition shape, and pillar traversal. Founder's 4 canonical use cases anchor each tier.

TierNameOperator use caseTime horizonPrimary pillars
T0Atomic"Do this small task — research / fetch info from the web"seconds-minutesB1 → B2 → B3c
T1Multi-action"Fetch data from relevant tools, communicate to people, operationalize it"minutes-hoursB1 → B2 → B3b → B4 → B5.a
T2Iterative"Here are instructions — show me 1-minute iterations; keep going"minutes (real-time loop)B1 → B3a → B3c → B1 refinement loop
T3Charter"I have an idea — bring it out. It has goals + objectives, multiple ways to meet, decisions involved, collaborative over time"days-monthsCharter materializes; all pillars in service of Charter goals

Tiers feed downstream: T3 (Charter) decomposes into T0-T2 sub-tasks. Method selection (D.11) chooses different intelligence per tier; Charter Model (D.10) defines fixed-vs-flexible scope for T3.

A.8 Actor Taxonomy #

Who appears on the operator's stage when CoS is running. Hidden implementation actors (Native platform internals — agents, charters-as-runtime, tools, state machines) live in Phase 2 design.

#ActorRole on operator's stageFirst appears in
AC1Operator (the founder)Asks, decides, reviews, approves. Principal of every Charter.All pillars
AC2CoS (Native-deployed)The implementation consultant. Frames work, executes end-to-end, surfaces decisions, closes the loop. Acts on operator's behalf within Charter bounds.All pillars
AC3External humansPeople CoS talks to on operator's behalf — board, team, vendor, customer, regulator. Audience-calibrated per D.7.B4
AC4Peer Sutras v2+Other operators' deployed instances, federated under Authority.B2.c (read) + B4 (peer-A2A v2+)

Not on this stage — Native platform internals (agent processes, charter runtime, tool bindings, state envelopes) are deliberately hidden from the operator surface. They live in Phase 2 design + the separate Native platform PRD when written.

A.9 Customer Skill Modes — how CoS adapts surface to each operator #

Operators show up with different levels of clarity about what they want. CoS adapts its working mode per task (could be different across tasks for the same operator). Skill mode is detected from the operator's utterance, prior patterns (B5.d), and Charter scope.

Skill modeCustomer stateCoS working modeAuthority default
SM1 — Idea only"I want X but I don't know the output shape or how to get there"High-touch. CoS proposes output shape + asks for ratification + then auto-executes. B3a THINK heavy.Conservative — ask before any external effect.
SM2 — Idea + output"I want X, should look like Y, don't know how"Medium-touch. CoS pure auto-execute path + presents Y. B3b DO heavy.Default Authority Model (D.6) thresholds.
SM3 — Idea + output + how"I want X, looks like Y, here's how — execute"Low-touch. CoS pure execution + delivers. Operator's HOW manifest used directly.Aggressive — operator's HOW signals trust.

Example customer mapping

  • Testlify CEO — primarily SM2 (knows desired output formats; relies on CoS for how). Sometimes SM1 for new strategic asks.
  • Dharmik Chief of Staff — primarily SM3 (highly operational; defined workflows). Sometimes SM2 for novel asks.
  • Healthtech PM — mix of SM1 and SM2 depending on whether discovery or execution.
  • Neurics head-of-sales co-founder — primarily SM2 for sales workflows; SM1 for strategic decisions.

Cross-references — Skill mode detection feeds HOW Engine (§3) detailing dimension. Authority gate (D.6) defaults per skill mode. Operator satisfaction signals (§7) include "skill-mode misdetection rate" — when CoS reads SM2 but operator was actually SM1.

§2.B Pillars (5) — CoS operator surface #

Five operator demands in the operator's own words. Each pillar absorbs needs and connects via the three views. Engineering detail lives in §4 Native Platform (primitives) and the Phase 2 design doc.

§2.B.0 Pillar grouping — how the 5 pillars form one system

Pillars are not 5 separate things; they form one cognitive cycle around the operator. Grouped by axis:

                       OPERATOR  (person in the company)
                                 │
       ┌─────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────┐
       │                                                   │
       ▼                                                   ▼
   ┌────────────┐                                  ┌────────────────┐
   │  INPUT     │   B1  "Ask my way"               │   OUTPUT       │
   │  axis      │   intent capture                 │   axis         │
   └─────┬──────┘                                  └────────┬───────┘
         │                                                  ▲
         ▼                                                  │
   ┌────────────┐    ┌────────────┐    ┌─────────────┐      │
   │  READ      │───▶│  ACTION    │───▶│  TALK       │──────┤  B4
   │  axis      │    │  axis      │    │  axis       │      │  "Talk to my
   │  B2        │    │  B3        │    │  B4         │      │   people"
   │  "World's  │    │  THINK+DO  │    │  "Talk for  │      │
   │   best"    │    │  +PRESENT  │    │   me"       │      │
   └─────┬──────┘    └─────┬──────┘    └──────┬──────┘      │
         │                 │                  │             │
         └────────┬────────┴──────────────────┘             │
                  ▼                                          │
   ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┴────┐
   │  MEMORY axis  ·  B5  "Remember, keep mine"  ·  system-of-record│
   │  Every other pillar writes here; B2 reads from here at intake.│
   └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

   Axes: Input (B1) · Read (B2) · Action (B3) · Output/Talk (B4) · Memory (B5)
   Grouping: B1+B2 = receive · B3 = process · B4 = emit · B5 = persist

The 5 axes are not arbitrary slots — they're the operator's full cognitive loop. A complete CoS turn touches each axis once.

PillarOperator demandAxis
B1"Let me ask in my own words"Input
B2"Bring me the world's best thinking, plus my own"Context (read path)
B3"Think with me where I must; do the rest yourself, end-to-end"Action (decide + execute + present)
B4"Talk to my people and the world for me"External communication
B5"Remember, explain, and keep it mine"Governance (write path · system-of-record)

Per-pillar shape: every pillar carries the same 6-block table grouped into three rings — SURFACE (operator-voice: Problem space · Need / outcome · Solution direction) CONTRACT (CoS's commitments: Product · Acceptance) MECHANISM (how it's built on Native platform: Connectedness pointer to Phase 2 design). Founder fills SURFACE; Claude details CONTRACT + MECHANISM.

Ring Traversal — how a single turn flows through the pillars

One operator utterance traverses all 5 pillars in a typical T1 multi-action task. Refinement loops short-circuit (operator → B3a refinement, not fresh B1). T3 Charter tasks materialize sub-tasks that each traverse this same flow.

   OPERATOR utterance
        │
        ▼
   ┌─────────────────┐     "Let me ask in my own words"
   │  B1  Ask my way │     (CoS parses intent — typed intent + sub-intents)
   └────────┬────────┘
            │ intent_type
            ▼
   ┌─────────────────────────┐   "Bring me the world's best + mine"
   │  B2  Context (read)     │   (CoS reads from B5 + world's best + peer Sutras v2+)
   │   B2.a my own           │
   │   B2.b world's best     │
   │   B2.c peer Sutras v2+  │
   └────────────┬────────────┘
                │ context envelope
                ▼
   ┌──────────────────────────────────┐  "Think with me where I must; do the rest"
   │  B3  Action (decide + execute)   │  (Authority gate via D.6; Method routing via D.11)
   │   B3a THINK   B3b DO   B3c PRESENT│
   └────┬──────────────────┬──────────┘
        │ output            │ comms needed
        │                   ▼
        │             ┌─────────────────────────┐  "Talk to my people for me"
        │             │  B4  External comms     │  (Connector layer; audience-calibrated)
        │             └────────────┬────────────┘
        │                          │ outbound + inbound responses
        ▼                          ▼
   ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────┐  "Remember, keep mine"
   │  B5  Governance (write — system-of-record)    │  (Every step logged; replayable; per-Charter)
   │   B5.a active  B5.b in-flight  B5.c audit     │
   │   B5.d learned                                 │
   └───────────────────────────────────────────────┘
            │
            └──→ feeds back into B2 future context (closes the loop)

   Refinement loop: operator → B3a (not B1)
   Retry loop:      B3 → B2 (more context needed)
   T3 Charter:      sub-tasks materialize, each traverses B1→B5

Cross-cutting overlays applying to B1

Operator-First Lens (D.8): Native parses for the operator's mental model, not a canonical form.

CX Bar (D.7): input must feel like talking to a smart COS, not filling a form.

Solution directionAwaiting founder fill. T-G1-04

ProductIntent Layer parses freeform utterance into typed intent + sub-intents. Show-by-example: operator demonstrates a task once, Native re-runs it. Trusted-names shortcut.
AcceptanceOperator types freeform intent; Native returns parsed intent_type + structured sub-intents within one turn; pre-declared output schema shown before LLM call; post-LLM output validated and rejected if non-conforming.

B2.a — My own v1 #

My catalog · my Charters · my decisions · my history · my entities. Read from B5 (system-of-record).

B2.b — World's best v1 #

Market standards · best practices · industry expert frameworks · regulatory info · public knowledge. Fed by Research-on-the-fly (B6 block — see canon).

B2.c — Peer Sutras v2+ #

Deferred to v2+. Other people's Native instances as context source — their knowledge · skills · methods · frameworks · thinking. Governed by federation protocol + trust + boundary. Phase 2 design: sutra-federation-design.md.

B3 — "Think with me where I must; do the rest yourself, end-to-end" #

Cross-cutting overlays applying to B3

Authority Model (D.6): default-go vs ask gated by 6 dimensions.

CX Bar: brainstorm interruptions are rare; routine work doesn't interrupt; output is scannable.

Operator-First Lens: "what matters now across my N companies" — exec triage.

The action axis. Internally decomposed into three modes — THINK (brainstorm partnership + iterative refinement), DO (auto-execute end-to-end), PRESENT (operator-facing output).

Ring 1Surface — operator voice
Problem spaceAwaiting founder fill. T-G1-08
Need / outcomeAwaiting founder fill. T-G1-09
Solution directionAwaiting founder fill. T-G1-10
Ring 2Contract — Native's commitments
ProductNative judges where operator-thinking is needed; partners on brainstorm; auto-executes the rest end-to-end (idea → operationalized standing pattern). Smart escalation: auto when path is known, ask when material/risky/ambiguous, remember the answer, operationalize from that point.
AcceptanceEvery multi-step task progresses through Task Framework phases; operator can inspect current phase + factors[]; phase transitions recorded; trivial edits auto-apply, material edits gated by Authority Model.
Ring 3Mechanism — how it's built (Phase 2)
ConnectednessConsumes B1 (intent) + B2 (context) + B5 (past-decisions). Triggers B4 when external comms needed. Writes to B5. Operator → B3 refinement loop (not a fresh B1 ASK). B3 → B2 retry loop. Phase 2 design: action-engine-design.md. T-G3-03
Interaction strip — B3 operator ↔ CoS 
  (no operator action — runs on B1+B2)
                                         │

                framing, competitive wedge, regulatory window. Brainstorm?"

B3a — THINK #

Brainstorm partnership; identifies "this is a brainstorm decision" moments; partners with operator on ideation. Iterative refinement is first-class: operator says "adjust this not that" — Native treats it as refinement on existing context, not a fresh ASK.

B3b — DO #

Auto-execute end-to-end when path is known. Idea → plan → execute → standing pattern (operationalization). Method-routing: different problem classes get different solving strategies.

B3c — PRESENT #

Operator-facing output rendered for decision-speed. Scannable. Actionable. Native's findings surfaced in a format that supports the operator's "make decision and move" mode.

B4 — "Talk to my people and the world for me" #

Cross-cutting overlays applying to B4

Authority Model: who can Native talk to on operator's behalf, at what level, with what constraints.

CX Bar: audience-calibrated voice — board / founder / team / client / vendor each get different tone.

External communication execution. v1: actual transport via Connector layer (B17). v2+: peer-Native A2A transport for cross-Sutra coordination.

Ring 1Surface — operator voice
Problem spaceAwaiting founder fill. T-G1-11
Need / outcomeAwaiting founder fill. T-G1-12
Solution directionAwaiting founder fill. T-G1-13
Ring 2Contract — Native's commitments
ProductNative sends typed messages on operator's behalf via Connectors (Slack / email / web); audience-calibrated voice; outbound logged in DecisionProvenance. v1 includes log-only A2A stub; full A2A transport v2+.
AcceptanceConnector messages emit DecisionProvenance + Artifact rows; failure surfaces with retry, not silent loss; operator does not switch surfaces. Authority gated per stakeholder + tool + $-threshold.
Ring 3Mechanism — how it's built (Phase 2)
ConnectednessConsumes B2 (who, what context) + B3 (what to say). Inbound responses re-enter via B3 (next action). Writes to B5. Prior comms become B2 future context. Phase 2 design: connector-comms-design.md. T-G3-04

B5.a — Active session #

Minutes/hours. What's right-now in flight. Backs N22 session-continuity.

B5.b — Portfolio in-flight #

Hours/days. Multi-task state for the 5 in-flight tasks across N companies. Backs N24. Engineering hard-problem (token budget allocation + multi-thread state machine) deferred to Phase 2 design.

B5.c — Audit + replay #

Days/forever. DecisionProvenance + AUDIT surface. Backs N#8.

B5.d — Learned patterns #

Months/forever. Person Formation + pattern emergence (k=4). Backs N#5.

§2.C Needs (41 — N01-N24 original, clustered into 4 operator-stages; N25-N41 parity extension 2026-06-12, each citing its Sutra-parity source) #

Eight original needs from PRD v2 §2 + sixteen new from COS framing + F1-F6 fixes + extension dimensions (peer-Sutra federation, multi-task context evolution). Each need phrased as the operator's own ask. Grouped into 4 operator-stage clusters that mirror the cognitive loop in §2.B.0 Pillar grouping.

   │  N12 auto/ask/remember         ·   N13 brainstorm moments      │
   │  N16 pick best intelligence    ·   N20 what matters now        │
   │  N21 adjust autonomy on fly                                    │
   └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
                                │
                                ▼
   ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │  CLUSTER 4  ·  COORDINATE & CONTROL  (B4 + B5 axis — talk for │
   │                                       me; explain everything) │
   │  ─────────────────────────────────                             │

   Loop closure: CLUSTER 4 outputs feed back into CLUSTER 2's substrate.

Each need phrased as "As the operator, I need to...". Cluster names mirror the cognitive stage (capture intent → understand context → execute action → coordinate + control outcome).

Legend: from X = consumes X's output · TRIG = triggers conditionally · MEM = bidirectional memory · RECORD = unidirectional write · (no) = no direct dependency · — = self.

§2.E User Journey Modes — 5 states the operator experiences #

CoS experience changes shape across 5 operator states. Same product, different surface emphasis per state. The system supports all 5 concurrently for one operator (they overlap in time across companies).

§2.E.1 The 5 states

StateWhat's happeningDominant pillar emphasisCoS posture
S1 Single taskOperator asks one discrete thing; CoS completes one turn end-to-end.B1 → B2 → B3 → (B4 if comms) → B5Crisp · scannable output · low-touch
S2 Multi-taskOperator has 3-5 in-flight tasks across N companies; CoS keeps each thread coherent.B5.b portfolio in-flight heavyContext-switching support · "what's now" surfacing · interruption safety
S3 ProjectOne sustained effort over days/weeks (e.g., Paisa Q3 SOM positioning project).T3 Charter materializes; cross-pillar coordination over timeGoal-tracking · milestone updates · adapting plan as info arrives
S4 EngagementLong-running working relationship between operator + CoS for one company or one domain (months).B5.d learned patterns dominant · B5.a active session keeps continuityPersonalized · pre-empts operator · runs on operator's "voice"
S5 Whole-system runningOperator's full portfolio: multiple engagements, multiple projects, multiple tasks concurrent across all companies. Native + CoS managing the workforce at scale.All pillars active across all Charters · operator-facing workforce-health surface visible (§4 Native platform manages the supervisor process silently per §A.2.2 post-onboarding zero-terminal rule)Daily Pulse · cross-portfolio priority surfacing · workforce health visible

§2.E.2 State transitions

   S1 single task ──completes──▶ S2 multi-task (if 2+ tasks in flight)
   S2 multi-task ──promotes──▶  S3 project (when task graduates to Charter)
   S3 project ──concludes──▶    S2 multi-task OR S4 engagement (if recurring)
   S4 engagement ──scales──▶    S5 whole-system (when across portfolio)
   S5 ─────────────────────────▶ ALL OTHER STATES active concurrently

   Native platform (§4) tracks state per Charter + per task.
   CoS adapts surface per dominant state.

§2.E.3 What's needed per state (acceptance)

  • S1: ≤1-turn parse + execute + present (per T0/T1 acceptance criteria).
  • S2: zero lost-context on session restart · N24 "5 in-flight tasks straight" need satisfied.
  • S3: Charter milestone gates emit progress updates · operator can replay plan-evolution.
  • S4: B5.d learned patterns surface in B2 retrieval · operator's voice signal stays consistent across pillars (B4 audience-calibrated).
  • S5: Daily Pulse aggregates · cross-portfolio priority view available · workforce health (Charter status across all companies) visible.

Cross-references — State detection feeds §A.9 Skill Modes default (S1 task often SM2; S3 project often SM1 in early phase, SM2 mid, SM3 late). §3 HOW selection adjusts per state (S5 whole-system uses bigger decomposition + cascading depth). §7 Observability tracks per-state quality.

§2.F Product Design — end-to-end operator view #

Scope. This section is the COMPLETE operator-facing design of Native: input → intent → 5 journeys → output, with personalization, trust moments, errors, capability discovery, and lifecycle. Product-only — implementation details (kernel · surfaces · primitives · gates · connectors · storage) live in §4 Native Platform + §11 Tech Design Hub. LLDs per journey/component come as separate follow-ups.

A. Foundations — who, why, what success

Before any flow, three answers: who is the operator, what jobs do they hire Native for, what does winning look like at 30 / 60 / 90 days. Everything downstream in §2.F is justified by these three.

A.1 Personas

Native serves four operator archetypes. Same engine, same journeys — different dominant jobs and different tolerance for autonomy.

PersonaEssence (one line)Dominant jobs
P1 — Founder / CEO Runs a company, time-starved, decides constantly, hates ceremony. Offload research + drafting + tracking. Capture directions before they evaporate. Surface what needs decision today.
P2 — Ops Lead Runs the day inside a team, owns recurring processes, lives in checklists. Convert one-off asks into standing routines. Watch SLAs. Reduce the "did this get done?" tax.
P3 — Solo Creator One-person shop. Builder + marketer + support + bookkeeper. Compress role-switching cost. Pick up dropped threads. Keep a single trustworthy memory of the business.
P4 — COO of T3 Client Senior operator inside a client co (Testlify, Dharmik) using Native through Sutra. Run cross-functional ops without re-briefing each ask. Get answers their team can act on. Show their CEO traction.

P1 + P3 share intensity-of-context. P2 + P4 share volume-of-recurrence. Native must serve both shapes from the same surface.

A.2 Jobs-to-be-done

The operator hires Native for five jobs. Each is phrased in their voice.

  • JTBD-1 (Task). When I have a concrete thing to get done and don't want to do it myself, I want Native to run it end-to-end so that my hour goes to the next thing, not this one.
  • JTBD-2 (Query). When I need to know something — a fact, a status, a synthesis — I want Native to return a trustworthy answer with its sources so that I can decide without a research detour.
  • JTBD-3 (Directive). When I want how-things-work to change going forward, I want Native to capture the rule once and apply it from then on so that I don't re-explain my preferences every session.
  • JTBD-4 (Feedback). When the output was wrong, off, or could be better, I want Native to absorb the correction and not repeat the miss so that the system gets sharper instead of just busier.
  • JTBD-5 (New Idea). When I have a half-formed thought, I want Native to hold it, sharpen it, and surface it back at the right moment so that ideas stop dying in my notes app.
A.3 North Star — OHS/wk (Operator-Hours Saved per Week)

One number. How many hours of operator time did Native take off the operator's plate this week — by doing work, returning answers, or preventing rework.

OHS/wk is measured per operator, not per company. It counts only work Native completed to operator satisfaction (no rework loop in the next 24h). Hours the operator spent supervising or correcting Native are subtracted.

WindowOHS/wk target bandWhat "good" feels like
Day 303 – 6 hrs/wk"It handles a handful of recurring asks. I check its work."
Day 608 – 12 hrs/wk"It owns several standing routines. I spot-check, don't re-run."
Day 9015 – 20 hrs/wk"It runs a real slice of my day. I notice when it's offline."
Why OHS/wk and not message count, task count, or "engagement". Volume metrics reward Native for being chatty. OHS/wk rewards Native for actually removing work. If Native sends 100 messages and saves 0 hours, OHS/wk says zero — correctly.
A.4 Success criteria at 30 / 60 / 90

What the operator can do, see, and feel at each stage. If any row is false, that stage hasn't shipped for that operator.

DAY 30  ── Trust earned for low-risk work
            ├── Operator runs ≥5 distinct ask types per week
            ├── Native completes ≥80% without a re-do
            ├── Operator has issued ≥1 standing directive that fires correctly
            └── Daily Pulse opened ≥3 days/wk

DAY 60  ── Routines own themselves
            ├── ≥3 standing routines fire without prompt
            ├── Feedback applied within same session ≥90% of time
            ├── Operator delegates a recurring weekly task end-to-end
            └── At least one "I would have forgotten that" save logged

DAY 90  ── Native is part of the operating rhythm
            ├── OHS/wk in 15-20 band for two consecutive weeks
            ├── Operator references Native in conversations with their team
            ├── Capability discovery surfaces ≥1 new use the operator adopts
            └── Drop-off risk indicators flat or declining

Foundations close here. Section B picks up at the moment the operator opens the input.

B. Input & Intent — from utterance to journey

Every Native interaction starts with one moment: the operator says something, Native decides what it is. Get that moment wrong and the rest of the journey is wasted motion. This section covers how input arrives, how Native reads intent, what carries across turns, and the trust handshake when Native isn't sure.

B.1 Input modalities

Native accepts input through channels the operator already uses. No new app, no new habit.

ModalityStatusBest forOperator example
Text chatDay 1Default for everything — explicit, replay-able, easy to correct."Draft the partner-update email for this week."
Slack mentionDay 1In-team asks, async hand-offs, capturing a direction mid-meeting."@native track this churn risk and tell me Friday."
Email forwardDay 1Hand off an inbound thread for Native to summarize, reply-draft, or file.Forward investor email with subject "what's the ask here?"
Scheduled promptDay 1Standing routines firing on a clock — the operator wrote the prompt once."Every Monday 8am: pulse of last week + top 3 today."
VoiceFutureHands-busy capture, car / walk / commute, raw thought dump."Hey Native, remind me to follow up with Benzy on Paisa pricing."
ScreenshotFuture"What does this mean / what should I do with this" against a captured screen.Screenshot of error toast — "is this the same one as yesterday?"
B.2 Intent recognition — the 5 types

Every utterance resolves to one of five intent types. The tell is what distinguishes one from another even when the words look similar.

IntentExample utteranceThe tell
Task"Draft a reply to Vinit's last email."
"Pull the Q2 revenue from the sheet and put it in the deck."		Concrete verb + concrete object. Operator wants a thing done, not learned.
Query"What did we decide about Paisa pricing last week?"
"How many T4 users opened the plugin yesterday?"		Question shape OR "tell me / find me / what's". Wants an answer, not an action.
Directive"From now on, always cite sources in research output."
"Stop emailing me on weekends unless it's urgent."		"From now on", "always", "never", "going forward". Changes future behavior, not this turn.
Feedback"That summary missed the actual decision."
"Too long. Cut by half next time."		References Native's prior output. Implicit or explicit "you did X — do Y instead".
New Idea"What if Native could read my calendar and pre-brief every meeting?"
"Explore using OHS/wk to score departments."		"What if", "explore", "could we", "I'm wondering". Not a request to act yet — a request to think.
B.3 Multi-turn context — what carries across

Native is not stateless. Across turns in the same session and across sessions in the same week, the following persist:

  • Recent intent. The last 3-5 things the operator asked, so "do that again for the other client" resolves.
  • Named entities. People, companies, projects the operator has talked about — so "Vinit" doesn't need a re-introduction.
  • Active charter. Standing directives the operator has issued (tone, channels, off-hours rules, who they trust on what).
  • Deferred follow-ups. Things Native promised to surface later ("remind me Friday") — they re-enter context at the right moment, not earlier.
  • Open questions. If Native asked the operator something and they didn't answer, that question stays warm for the next turn.
B.4 The trust moment — confirm-back below threshold

When Native is confident, it acts. When it isn't, it confirms before it acts. The operator never gets surprised by what Native thought they meant.

ConfidenceWhat operator sees
High (clear intent + clear object)Native proceeds. Confidence shown only if operator has asked to see it.
Medium (intent clear, object ambiguous)"Got it — you mean the Paisa partner email, not the Testlify one?" Yes/no, then proceed.
Low (intent itself unclear)"I read this as a task (draft the email) — is that right, or did you want me to just find the last one?"
Very low (can't parse)"I'm not sure what to do with this. Are you asking me to act, answer, or remember something?"
B.5 Input → intent → journey (fan-out)
OPERATOR UTTERANCE
        │
        ▼
   INTENT READ ────────► confidence high? ──no──► CONFIRM-BACK ──► (operator answers)
        │                                                │
        ▼                                                ▼
  ┌─────┴─────┬─────────┬───────────┬─────────┬──────────────┐
  │           │         │           │         │              │
TASK       QUERY    DIRECTIVE    FEEDBACK   NEW IDEA      UNKNOWN
"do X"    "tell Y"  "from now"  "that was   "what if      "I'm not
                                  wrong"     we..."        sure"
  │           │         │           │         │              │
  ▼           ▼         ▼           ▼         ▼              ▼
 §C.1       §C.2      §C.3        §C.4      §C.5         ASK BACK

C. The 5 journeys — operator-facing flows

A journey is what the operator lives through from utterance to outcome. Each of the 5 intent types has its own shape, its own approval moments, its own success and failure surfaces. Same engine underneath — different choreography on top.

C.1 TASK journey — "do X for me"

Typical utterance. "Draft the partner update for this week." / "Move the Friday standup to Thursday and send the new invite."

Flow:

  1. Operator states the task.
  2. Native reads intent + scope — names the deliverable in one line back ("OK, drafting the weekly partner update covering Paisa + Testlify").
  3. If the task is reversible and low-risk: Native proceeds. If irreversible (sending email, deleting, charging, scheduling external): Native produces the artifact and asks for go-ahead before the external action.
  4. Native does the work, surfaces the result in-channel.
  5. Operator approves, edits, or rejects. Edits feed forward into the next similar task (see §C.4).
  6. Native confirms completion in one line ("Sent. Logged under partner-updates.").

Branches.

  • Confidence low: Native confirms scope before starting ("I have two partner lists — Paisa investors or T4 users?").
  • Native disagrees / can't do it: Native says so plainly ("I don't have access to the invoice tool yet — want me to draft the message and you send it?"). No fake completion.
  • Operator changes mind mid-flow: "Stop, do it differently" cancels the in-flight task and restarts from the corrected ask. The half-finished draft is kept for one turn so the operator can salvage parts.

Success. Operator sees the finished artifact + a one-line "what I did" summary. Time-to-result is in seconds for drafting, minutes for multi-step.

Failure. Native states what it tried, where it stopped, and what it needs to finish — never silent, never fabricated. "I drafted the email but couldn't pull the Q2 number — sheet access denied. Paste the number and I'll finish."

C.2 QUERY journey — "tell me Y"

Typical utterance. "What did we decide about Paisa pricing last week?" / "How many T4 users used the plugin yesterday?"

Flow:

  1. Operator asks.
  2. Native picks the source — memory, prior session, connected data, web — and declares it.
  3. Native returns the answer with confidence level (high / moderate / low / unknown) and sources named inline.
  4. If the answer needs synthesis across sources, Native shows the synthesis + a "based on" line.
  5. Native offers one follow-up branch if obvious ("Want me to draft the reply to Benzy about this?").

Branches.

  • Confidence low: Native returns the partial answer and labels what it doesn't know ("I see two pricing discussions — I'm not sure which you mean. Both are below.").
  • Native can't find it: "I don't have a record of that decision. Want me to check your email / Slack / docs?" — never invents.
  • Operator changes mind: "Actually, just give me the Testlify number instead" — Native drops the prior answer, re-runs against the new ask.

Success. Answer + source + confidence, on one screen, no hunting.

Failure. "I don't know" stated plainly, with what Native checked and what's left to try. No fabrication, no padding.

C.3 DIRECTIVE journey — "from now on, do Z"

Typical utterance. "From now on, always cite sources." / "Stop emailing me on weekends unless it's urgent."

Flow:

  1. Operator issues the directive.
  2. Native reads scope — one-time vs standing, this project vs all projects, this session vs forever.
  3. Native confirms scope back in one line ("Got it — applies to all research output, across every project, until you say otherwise. Logged.").
  4. Native stores it in the operator's charter (a visible, editable list — see §E).
  5. Next time a matching context appears, Native applies the directive and shows a small marker ("applied: cite-sources").
  6. Operator can list, edit, or revoke any standing directive in one ask ("show my standing rules" / "drop the weekend rule").

Branches.

  • Confidence low (scope unclear): Native asks one disambiguation — "Just for Paisa or everywhere?"
  • Native sees a conflict with an existing directive: Native surfaces both ("This conflicts with 'always email me daily summaries'. Drop the old one, or keep both with weekend exception?").
  • Operator changes mind: "Actually, just for this week" — Native re-scopes to time-bounded and notes the expiry.
DIRECTIVE FLOW

operator: "from now on, X"
        │
        ▼
   scope read ──── one-time? ──yes──► apply now, don't store
        │
        no (standing)
        │
        ▼
   confirm scope ──conflict with charter?──yes──► surface both, ask
        │                                              │
        no                                             │
        ▼                                              ▼
   add to charter ◄──────────────── operator picks resolution
        │
        ▼
   future contexts ──► apply + show marker "applied: X"
        │
        ▼
   anytime: "show rules" / "drop X" / "edit X"

Success. Directive is visible in the charter, fires automatically the next time it applies, marker shows the operator it fired.

Failure. Native captured the directive but couldn't apply it (capability gap) — surfaces the gap and parks the rule until the capability exists.

C.4 FEEDBACK journey — "that was wrong, try again"

Typical utterance. "That summary missed the actual decision." / "Too long, cut by half next time."

Flow:

  1. Operator corrects.
  2. Native reads what changed: format, content, tone, scope, or accuracy.
  3. Two-track response: now (re-do this output applying the correction) and future (carry the correction forward to similar work).
  4. Native re-runs the immediate output and surfaces it.
  5. Native names what it's carrying forward ("Got it — keeping summaries to ~5 lines for partner updates going forward.").
  6. Operator confirms the future-rule sticks, or scopes it down ("just for this one, not standing").

Branches.

  • Confidence low (what to change is unclear): Native asks one targeted question — "Cut what — the context section, or the action items too?"
  • Native disagrees with the correction: Native says so once, with reason, then complies — "I included the decision because it changed mid-week, but cutting it now. Flag if you want it back."
  • Operator changes mind: "Actually, the first version was right" — Native restores and unlearns the queued correction.

Success. Immediate redo lands; the same miss does not repeat in the next 24 hours on similar work. Operator sees both what changed now and what changed forward.

Failure. If Native can't tell what to change, it asks once and stops — does not loop guessing.

C.5 NEW IDEA journey — "I want a new capability"

Typical utterance. "What if Native could pre-brief every meeting from my calendar?" / "Explore using OHS/wk to score departments."

Flow:

  1. Operator floats the idea.
  2. Native does NOT immediately build or commit. It enters ideation mode — reflects the idea back in sharper words, names the question underneath, lists 2-3 shapes the idea could take.
  3. Operator picks a shape, parks it, or drops it.
  4. If parked: idea goes into the operator's parking lot — visible, named, dated.
  5. Native nudges the parked idea back at a relevant moment ("You parked 'pre-brief meetings' two weeks ago — calendar access just landed. Want to try it?").
  6. If the operator commits: Native proposes a small first version + what success looks like before any build.

Branches.

  • Confidence low (idea is vague): Native asks two sharpening questions — "Pre-brief with what? Last meeting notes, or open threads with that person?"
  • Native sees this is already a capability: Native says so + shows how to use it now ("This already runs as Daily Pulse — want me to add the meeting layer?").
  • Operator changes mind: "Drop it" — idea leaves the parking lot; Native confirms the drop so it doesn't resurface.
NEW IDEA FLOW

operator: "what if..."
        │
        ▼
   reflect back ──► sharpen ──► show 2-3 shapes
        │
        ├──► COMMIT ──► propose small v1 + success criteria ──► build
        │
        ├──► PARK ───► add to parking lot ──► nudge later when relevant
        │
        └──► DROP ───► confirm + don't resurface

Success. The idea is either alive (committed with a v1 in motion), warm (parked with a clear re-surface trigger), or cleanly killed (dropped, won't resurface). Never lost.

Failure. Native can't find a viable shape for the idea — surfaces that plainly ("I don't see how this fits today — I'll park it with a note and check back in two weeks") rather than pretending to commit.

D. Outputs & Channels — how Native talks back

Operator hires Native to do work, not to send messages. Output discipline: land the right shape, on the right channel, at the right time. Default to least-noise, escalate only when the operator's stated cadence or the stakes demand it.

D.1 Response modalities

Six shapes. Native picks one (sometimes two) per response. Operator can pin a default per ask type or per channel.

ModalityShapeWhen Native picks it
M1 — Text replyInline answer in the same surface the operator opened.Default. Query answers, short task confirmations, conversational follow-ups.
M2 — Slack pingDM to operator or post to a named channel.Operator is not in chat right now and asked for Slack delivery, OR a standing routine completed and the team needs to see it.
M3 — EmailPlain email, subject line carries the headline.External-facing draft, long-form synthesis, anything that needs to live in a thread the operator forwards.
M4 — Summary cardCompact card: headline · 3 bullets · one link · timestamp.Status update after a multi-step task. Daily Pulse entries. "Here's what I did" closers.
M5 — Approval-ask cardCard with the planned action, the reversibility note, and Approve / Adjust / Cancel.Native is about to do something with real-world side effects the operator hasn't pre-blessed.
M6 — Scheduled reminderFuture ping at a stated time, carrying the original context.Operator said "remind me", or Native is holding an idea until the right moment per Journey 5.
D.2 Sync, scheduled, async

Three landing times. Operator feels the difference; Native picks based on the ask shape.

  • Sync. Same turn, same surface. Queries and quick tasks. Operator is waiting.
  • Scheduled. Lands at a stated moment (08:00 digest, "ping me Friday", "remind me when the doc is signed"). Operator is not waiting; the clock or a trigger is.
  • Async. Lands when the work finishes — could be 30 seconds or 30 minutes. Long tasks, research, multi-step routines. Native confirms receipt up front so the operator knows it's underway.
D.3 Channel selection

Three rules, applied in order. First match wins.

  1. Operator said where. If the ask carries an explicit channel ("send to Slack #ops", "email this to me", "DM me when done"), use that.
  2. Standing preference. If the operator has set a default for this ask type or this time-of-day, use that.
  3. Default to origin. Otherwise, reply on the surface the ask came in on. Chat-in → chat-out. Email-in → email-out.

Escalation override: if the matter is time-sensitive AND the default channel is async, Native promotes the channel one step (chat → Slack DM → SMS-tier ping) and notes why.

D.4 Modality routing
ASK COMES IN
   |
   v
Explicit channel stated? ── YES ──> Use it
   |
   NO
   v
Standing preference for  ── YES ──> Use it
this ask type set?
   |
   NO
   v
Default: reply on origin channel
   |
   v
Pick modality by ask shape
   |
   +-- Quick answer ........... M1 Text
   +-- Status after work ...... M4 Summary card
   +-- Side-effect action ..... M5 Approval-ask card
   +-- External-facing draft .. M3 Email
   +-- Future-time delivery ... M6 Scheduled reminder
   +-- Team needs to see it ... M2 Slack
   |
   v
Time-sensitive AND channel
async? ─── YES ──> Escalate one step + note reason
   |
   NO
   v
DELIVER

E. Personalization & Memory

Native gets sharper the longer an operator uses it. Two mechanisms: skill mode (how Native talks to this operator) and memory (what Native carries forward between sessions).

E.1 Skill modes

Three modes. Native infers the mode from how the operator phrases asks, how often they correct, and how much scaffolding they consume. Operator can set it explicitly ("treat me as expert on legal, novice on code").

ModeOperator feelHow Native adjusts
SM1 — Novice "Walk me through it. I want to understand before I approve." Fuller wording. Names the steps before doing them. Confirms before any irreversible action. Explains source and reasoning in answers. Offers the next-step suggestion explicitly.
SM2 — Intermediate "Tell me the headline, do the obvious thing, ask only when it forks." Compact wording. Confirms only at branches. Reports back with summary cards, not narration. Shows source on request, not by default.
SM3 — Expert "Just do it. Surface only what I would want to override." Terse output. Acts on reasonable defaults without confirmation. Logs what it did rather than asking permission. Confirms only for high-stakes or irreversible moves.

Mode is per-domain, not global. Same operator can be SM3 on calendar, SM1 on contracts. Native carries the per-domain reading and can show the operator their current map on request.

E.2 Charter & declared preferences

The charter is what the operator has explicitly told Native about how they want to be served. Native treats charter entries as durable until revoked.

  • Cadence. When the operator wants to hear from Native — daily digest time, quiet hours, weekly retro window.
  • Hands-off level. Per ask class, how much autonomy is granted. ("Calendar: just do it. Money out: always ask.")
  • Communication style. Length tolerance, formality, language, whether to lead with the recommendation or the rationale.
  • Channel defaults. Which modality lands where (see §D.3).
  • People & relationships. Who matters, who they are to the operator, how they should be addressed.
E.3 What Native remembers

Memory is layered. Each layer has different staying power and different visibility to the operator.

LayerHoldsLifespan
Working memoryThe current thread of asks, drafts in flight, unresolved clarifications.Session, with handoff to the next session if the thread is open.
Named entitiesPeople, companies, projects, products the operator has mentioned with a stable identity.Until the operator says forget, or the entity goes inactive for 90 days and the operator confirms archive.
Trusted namesWho can act on the operator's behalf, who is allowed to receive what.Durable. Changes require explicit operator confirmation.
Prior intentsWhat the operator has asked for before, with the result. Used for "do that again" and for capability discovery.Rolling. Older entries summarize; details fall off after the operator stops referencing them.
Ratified directivesStanding rules from Journey 3 — "from now on, X".Until revoked. Revocation is a single ask.
E.4 What Native forgets
  • Sensitive ephemeral content the operator marked one-time (passwords, OTPs, draft messages they discarded).
  • Expired charters — anything time-bounded ("for this quarter only") drops out when the window closes.
  • Anything the operator asks to forget. The forget command is honored immediately and visibly confirmed.
E.5 Curation surface

Operator can ask at any moment: "what do you remember about X?" and get back a structured view — entities, prior intents, standing directives, charter entries touching X. From the same view they can forget Y, correct Z, or pin this as durable. The operator never has to guess what Native is carrying.

F. Trust & Authority moments — when Native asks before acting

Native runs on a simple promise: act on what's safe and known, ask on what's risky or new. The operator should never be surprised by an action they didn't authorize, and never be nagged for an action they already blessed. Trust is the dial.

F.1 When Native asks before acting

Four trigger classes pull Native into "ask first" mode. Everything else, Native acts and reports.

TriggerWhat the operator seesWhy it asks
High-risk action "Before I send this to your full team, confirm — send / edit / cancel." Spend, send, share, or commit that touches people or money outside Native's own scope.
Irreversible action "This can't be undone after I run it. Type 'go' to confirm." Deletion, public publication, external notification, contract change. Two-step is the minimum.
New authority "You haven't asked me to do X before. Grant once / grant always / not now." First time Native is being asked to take an action it has no prior permission template for.
First-time channel or contact "First message to this person/channel — preview before I send?" Catch wrong-recipient and wrong-tone errors before they become artifacts.
F.2 Reversibility signals

Every action carries a visible reversibility tag so the operator knows what they're authorizing.

  • Reversible. "I can undo this for the next 5 minutes — say 'undo' anytime."
  • Reversible with effort. "Undoing this means I have to retract messages / re-issue / re-pay. Doable, not free."
  • Not reversible. "Once I do this, it's done. Confirming locks it in."
F.3 Audit visibility — "show me what you did and why"

At any moment, the operator can ask Native to replay any past action. The operator gets back: what was asked, what Native did, why it chose that path, what it would do differently with hindsight. No log-diving, no IT tickets — same chat surface as everything else.

F.4 Trust ladder over time

Native starts conservative and earns its way to higher autonomy. The operator can also dial up or dial down explicitly at any rung.

RUNG 1 (Day 0-14)   ── ask before every external action
RUNG 2 (Day 15-45)  ── auto-act on repeated patterns; ask on novel
RUNG 3 (Day 46-90)  ── auto-act on routines; ask only on high-risk/irreversible
RUNG 4 (Day 90+)    ── runs standing routines fully; surfaces what it did, not what it'll do
F.5 Ask-vs-act boundary (operator view)
operator ask
    |
    v
+---+-----------------------------+
| is this irreversible? ----yes--> ASK (multi-step confirm)
+---+-----------------------------+
    | no
    v
+---+-----------------------------+
| is this high-risk?      ----yes--> ASK (one-tap confirm)
+---+-----------------------------+
    | no
    v
+---+-----------------------------+
| new authority?          ----yes--> ASK (grant once / always / no)
+---+-----------------------------+
    | no
    v
+---+-----------------------------+
| first time on this channel? -yes-> ASK (preview before send)
+---+-----------------------------+
    | no
    v
   ACT, report after

G. Errors & Recovery — when things go sideways

Things break. Native's job in failure is the same as in success: be honest about what happened, give the operator a clean way forward, and remember the miss so it doesn't repeat.

G.1 Misclassified intent

Native thought the operator wanted X. Operator wanted Y. Before acting on anything ambiguous, Native surfaces its read.

operator: "send the wedding update to the family group"
native:   "i think you mean — draft + send to 'Family WhatsApp', not 'Family Email'. yes / no / clarify"
   |
   |-- yes ------> proceed
   |-- no -------> "tell me which group, or paste a name"
   |-- clarify --> native asks one focused question, no more
G.2 Failed action

The action couldn't complete. Native surfaces the failure plainly, in the operator's words, with three options. No stack traces, no codes.

Operator seesOptions
"Couldn't reach Slack just now." retry now · try again in 10 min · cancel
"The doc is locked by someone else." wait and retry · make a copy · cancel
"Half the contacts received it, half didn't." retry the misses · show me which · leave as-is
G.3 Operator override — stop, undo, different approach
  • "stop" — Native halts the in-flight action where it can, reports what completed and what didn't, asks nothing further.
  • "undo" — Native reverses the last action if reversible; if not, it says so and offers the next-best recovery (retract, re-send correction, manual revert path).
  • "different approach" — Native discards its current plan, re-reads the original ask, proposes a fresh plan, waits for go.
G.4 "Wrong answer, try again" loop

When the operator pushes back on a result, Native does not just re-roll. It asks what was wrong — facts, tone, scope, recipient, timing — applies the correction, and runs once more. Two consecutive misses on the same ask escalate: Native stops trying and asks the operator to walk it through what right looks like.

G.5 Learning from the correction

Every correction the operator gives is visible back to them — "I noted that. I won't make the same call on Slack channel naming next time." If the correction is general enough to be a standing rule, Native asks if the operator wants it captured as a directive. The miss is never silent and never repeated without surface.

H. Capability Discovery + Daily Pulse

Two affordances solve the same operator problem from different sides: I don't know what to ask for. Discovery answers it on demand. Daily Pulse answers it before the operator opens the surface.

H.1 "What can you do for me?"

Operator can ask the question in any phrasing and get a structured capability map — not a feature list. The map is organized by job, not by tool, and is filtered to what's relevant to this operator's domain and stage.

ViewWhat the operator sees
By jobGrouped under the five JTBDs — Task, Query, Directive, Feedback, New Idea — with concrete example asks for each.
By domainGrouped by area of the operator's life — calendar, inbox, research, drafting, hiring, finance, people — showing what Native already touches and what it can pick up.
By stageWhat's live for this operator today, what's available but unused, what unlocks once the operator grants a connection or sets a preference.
H.2 Proactive capability surfacing

Native does not dump the catalog. It surfaces a capability at the moment it would have helped. Three triggers:

  • Adjacent ask. Operator just finished a task; an adjacent capability would close the loop. ("I drafted the email — want me to schedule the follow-up too?")
  • Repeated manual. Operator has done the same manual sequence three times. Native offers to take it over as a standing routine.
  • Dormant fit. Operator hasn't used a capability that matches their current goal. Surfaced once, gently, with an opt-in.

Capability suggestions never block. They appear as a soft offer next to the current output, and decay if the operator declines twice.

H.3 Daily Pulse

Lands at the operator's stated morning time. One screen. Designed to be skimmed in under 60 seconds and acted on with one tap per item.

+----------------------------------------------------------+
| DAILY PULSE                          Mon 08:00            |
+----------------------------------------------------------+
| YESTERDAY ── done                                         |
|   3 routines fired clean · 2 drafts sent · 1 saved hour   |
|                                                           |
| OPEN ── needs you                                         |
|   1) Approve send: vendor renewal reply  [Approve][Edit]  |
|   2) Pick: 2 meeting times for Anjali    [A][B][Other]    |
|                                                           |
| SUGGESTED ── if you want                                  |
|   - Convert "Friday investor update" to standing routine? |
|   - Forward weekend signups to your Monday review?        |
|                                                           |
| SIGNAL ── this week                                       |
|   OHS:  11.4 hrs / target 12   ▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓▓░                |
|   Re-do rate:  6%              good                       |
|                                                           |
| ASK ANYTHING                                              |
+----------------------------------------------------------+

Four blocks, fixed order. Yesterday earns trust by showing closed loops. Open is the only block that asks for the operator's attention — each item is one tap to clear. Suggested is the proactive surface from H.2. Signal shows the OHS/wk reading and the rework rate so the operator can feel Native getting better. The operator can dismiss any block permanently, reorder them, or collapse the Pulse to a single-line headline.

H.4 Weekly retro

Once a week, at a quieter moment, Native sends a longer-form retro: what got done, what got dropped, which directives fired most, which suggestions the operator accepted, and one proposed change to how Native is serving them. The retro is the operator's chance to retune the charter without having to ask for the meeting.

I. Lifecycle — onboarding to mature operator

The operator's relationship with Native moves through five stages. Each stage has a shape, a felt experience, and a failure mode if Native gets it wrong.

I.1 First session (~10 minutes)

The very first conversation does three things in this order: a light charter setup (who the operator is, what their company does, what they spend their day on), a quick persona read (P1 founder, P2 ops lead, P3 solo, P4 COO-at-client), and a first real job. Not a tutorial — a real ask the operator was going to do anyway. By minute 10, Native has completed one piece of actual work and the operator has felt the loop once.

I.2 Day 1

What shipped: the first job from session one, plus 1-3 quick follow-ups the operator threw at it the rest of the day. What's queued: standing directives the operator mentioned but haven't yet fired ("from now on, summarize my Slack catch-up every morning" — captured, scheduled, not yet run).

I.3 Week 1

The charter ratifies — Native has enough texture on the operator to stop asking foundational questions. The first 2-3 standing directives ratify and start firing on schedule. The first Daily Pulse lands and the operator opens it. By end of week, the operator has used at least three distinct ask types and re-used at least one.

I.4 Month 1

The mature loop is established: ask, act, report, learn. OHS/wk lands in the 3-6 band. The operator has had at least one "Native saved me from a mistake" moment — a wrong recipient caught, a fact corrected, a forgotten follow-up surfaced in time. Native runs ≥1 standing routine end-to-end without prompt.

I.5 Day 90+ (mature)

Habit-formed. Native runs a real slice of the operator's day. OHS/wk lives in the 15-20 band. The operator references Native in conversations with their team ("Native sent that") — the most reliable signal that it's part of how they work, not a tool they visit.

I.6 Drop-off triggers + re-engagement
Drop-off signalNative's recovery move
Daily Pulse unopened 3 days in a rowShrink pulse to 3 lines, re-anchor to highest-OHS item
No new asks in 5 daysSurface a dormant capability the operator hasn't tried, framed against a current pain
Operator started doing a task manually they used to delegateAsk one direct question: "you did X yourself yesterday — did I get it wrong before?"
Operator turned off a standing routineConfirm, log why, offer a leaner version next week
 
DAY 0      DAY 1     WEEK 1        MONTH 1            DAY 90+
  |          |         |              |                  |
first      first    charter +     mature loop +       habit-formed
session    real     directives    first save +        +20 OHS/wk +
+ first    follow-  ratify +      OHS 3-6 band        team-aware
job done   ups      Daily Pulse                       references
  |          |         |              |                  |
felt loop  reuse   rhythm forms   "I trust this"    "I rely on this"

If Native is doing its job, the operator stops thinking about Native and starts thinking about the work Native makes possible. That's the lifecycle's terminal state.

Next: LLDs per part. Each journey, each output channel, and each lifecycle stage gets its own LLD as separate follow-up cards. The implementation layer (Native runtime, surfaces, primitives, gates, connectors, storage) is in §4 + §11.

§3 HOW Methodology — design as Lenses #

PurposeNative's IP: not "do the work" — "do the work via the right way of looking at this task." The methodology that defines what a Lens is, what axes Native recommends, and how an operator decides which Lens to apply.
OwnsThe Lens primitive · axes (Realization + Reflection as the recommended pair; many others available) · the value-is-an-axis recursion · examples · the decision procedure · the operator-facing surface of HOW.
Depends on§2 task tier (T0-T3); §4 Native runtime (where Lens choices manifest); §7 Observability (where Lens accuracy is measured).
Does NOT ownExecution (§4 runtime); operator surface (§2); transport (§5); the DAG / build / Native-primitive implementation (Claude-suggested, archived — not committed design).

One sentence (theory). Every piece of design knowledge is a "way of looking" (a Lens). There are many possible lenses (axes), not capped to any number; the two recommended ones for building anything are Realization (abstract idea → running thing) and Reflection (instance → axis → meta-axis → … → grammar). You don't have to use all axes — you pick the lenses a destination actually needs. This section is THEORY work; productization is the next step.

§3.1 The atom — a value is just an axis

The smallest move is classification: a value is an item; an axis is a set of values. The role is RELATIVE to level — same thing is a value seen from above, an axis seen from below:

  a thing is a VALUE   seen from the axis above it
  the same thing is an AXIS    seen from the values below it

"Value" and "axis" are the same kind of object viewed from one level up or down. This is the only primitive; everything else is this move repeated. (Formal grounding: type-universe relativity; Tarski's object-language / meta-language stratification.)

§3.2 Axes — many possible, two recommended

An axis is a particular Lens — one way of looking at the thing being built. There can be MANY axes (viewpoint · concern · time · ownership · scale · certainty · …). You are not capped to any number. You pick the lenses that change a decision for this idea and ignore the rest.

The two we recommend for every idea:

AxisNameMeansExample values
Axis 1Realizationabstract idea → concrete running thingworldview · vision · strategy · capability · architecture · feature · contract · implementation
Axis 2Reflectioninstance → axis → meta-axis → method → framework → paradigm → grammar (the recursion, uncapped)M0 instance · M1 axis · M2 meta-axis · M3 method · M4 framework · M5 paradigm · M6 grammar

Other available lenses (add any per idea, only if it changes a decision): viewpoint (user / system / governance) · concern (security / cost / accuracy) · time (past / now / next / later · v1 / v2 / v3) · ownership (human / AI / human-in-loop) · scale (fleet / cohort / instance).

Coupling (how the two recommended axes interact): as you ascend Reflection to mint a structure (an axis, a schema), the forward Realization journey then consumes that structure instead of re-deriving it. Climb to write a schema once; descend and every instance conforms. The abstraction is carried forward as an input, not copied.

§3.3 Dynamic — nothing is capped

The number of Realization levels and Reflection heights is not fixed. It is decided per idea.

  • Dimensions are generated, not listed. Each dimension = (interrogative × mechanism) — a finite generator mints an open set on demand.
  • Realization stops by atomicity (downward): you stop decomposing at the smallest buildable unit.
  • Reflection stops by reflexivity (upward): you stop climbing at the level that describes itself.
  • Both stop by the decision test (the practical cap): add a level or a dimension only while it still changes what you build or who reads it.

Possibility is not obligation. The space is astronomic; you instantiate only the corner a destination needs.

§3.4 Examples (concrete + visual)

Example 1 — Realization axis (one idea, abstract → concrete):

   worldview     operator sovereignty (the operator owns their own work)
   vision        every operator has a Chief of Staff that classifies what they say
   strategy      agentic OS + governance above AI + classify before acting
   capability    classify the operator's intent from raw words
   architecture  B1 Intent block inside the CoS layer of Native
   feature       B1 intake (capture -> classify -> conf-gate -> log -> route)
   contract      intent { type, confidence, routed_to, clarify }
   implementation classifier prompt + intake_log.jsonl + emit

Example 2 — Time axis (same thing across time) — time is an available lens (not primary), shown as a valid demonstration of "another axis":

   past      (no formal intake; operator wrote slash-commands instead)
   v1 / now  single LLM call. Conf-gate at 0.6. Clarify-loop if below.
   v2 / next eval-tuned threshold (raised to 0.75); golden-set CI on every change.
   v3 / later voice + screenshot intent (multi-modal classifier).

Example 3 — Many axes (the nD nature) — same B1 intake through other lenses, any of which you can add when a decision needs them:

   viewpoint lens     user sees:      "Got it, task. Confirm? Slack at 5?"
                      system sees:    JSON event { type:task, conf:0.81 }
                      governance sees: low-risk · reversible · auto-approve
   concern lens       security · cost · accuracy (each with its own values)
   ownership lens     classify=AI · clarify=human-in-loop · provenance=human-readable

Example 4 — Realization × Reflection clean 6×7 cross (the recommended pair). What "Reflection" means here: the meta-ascent up the classification recursion — value → axis → meta-axis → method → framework → paradigm → reflexive grammar. NOT visibility; NOT the narrow CS sense; NOT the same as "going abstract" along the subject (that's Realization's job).

Realization ↓ / Reflection →M0 instanceM1 axisM2 meta-axisM3 methodM4 frameworkM5 paradigmM6 grammar
Worldview"operator sovereignty"founding principlevalue-system kindfirst-principles thinkingFounding Doctrineethicsdistinction
Vision"OHS/wk North Star"vision metricoutcome typeOKR / North-StarStrategic Visionleadership theoryinquiry
Strategy"governance above AI"strategy facetstrategy kindWardley mapstrategic craftstrategic managementrationality
Architecture"5 layers + 6 surfaces"architecture facetarchitecture styleC4 model4+1 views / Zachmanenterprise architecturesystems thinking
Feature"B1 intake block"feature facetfeature kindstory mapping / JTBDAgileproduct managementdesign thinking
Implementation"intake_log.jsonl + classifier prompt"implementation facetimplementation patternTDD / BDDXP / DDDsoftware engineeringcomputation

Read any row left→right as: a concrete thing → the axis it belongs to → the axis-of-axes → the method that produces them → the framework hosting the method → the paradigm → the reflexive grammar. The recursion is the same shape every time.

Example 5 — One-to-many on both sides. The 6×7 above is rendered "1 cell = 1 value" for readability; in real design every cell typically holds MULTIPLE values. Two examples:

5a — Expanding ONE column (M3 method) — many methods per Realization tier:

Realization tierM3 method (multiple per cell)
Worldviewfirst-principles thinking · deontological reasoning · virtue ethics · pragmatism
VisionOKRs · BHAG · North-Star metric · Vision Pyramid · Amazon PR-FAQ

Featurestory mapping · Jobs-to-be-Done · Kano model · user stories · MoSCoW
ImplementationTDD · BDD · XP · DDD · Hexagonal architecture · CQRS

5b — Expanding ONE row (Architecture) — many values per Reflection level (fan-out tree):

  Architecture  (one Realization row, expanded across all 7 Reflection levels)
   +-- M0 instance      +- 5-layer architecture
   |                    +- 6 surfaces (ROUTE/RUN/GATE/EMERGE/AUDIT/TENANT)
   |                    +- 10 primitives · 26 EngineEvents · 8 hardstops
   +-- M1 axis          +- structure axis · behavior axis · deployment axis
   +-- M2 meta-axis     +- monolithic <-> distributed
   |                    +- synchronous <-> asynchronous
   |                    +- stateful <-> stateless
   +-- M3 method        +- C4 · Zachman · 4+1 · ADR pattern
   +-- M4 framework     +- TOGAF · arc42 · 4+1 view model · Zachman
   +-- M5 paradigm      +- enterprise / service-oriented / microservices / event-driven
   +-- M6 grammar       +- systems thinking · cybernetics

The cross is many-to-many, not 1-to-1: each cell is a SET of candidates. A build decision is the act of picking one (or a few) from each relevant cell.

Example 6 — When more than 2 axes need to meet — how to capture it visually. You cannot draw n orthogonal axes flat. Established techniques:

TechniqueHow it shows >2 axesSource
Small multiplesa grid of 2D tables, one per value of the 3rd axisTufte
Parallel coordinatesn vertical axes side-by-side; each item is a polyline crossing allInselberg
Heatmap2 axes as grid; 3rd encoded as cell color/intensitystandard
Faceted filter / browsenever show all n; pick values on some axes, see the remaining slice — how nD is actually used in practiceRanganathan
Hierarchical nested headersmulti-level row/column headers stack two axes into one directionstandard table design
3D cube + slicescube for 3 axes; pull 2D slices and lay them out as small multiplesstandard

Punchline: you don't visualize nD directly. You project. Every diagram is a 2D projection of an n-dimensional address space. Pick the projection (2 or 3 axes) that answers the question at hand.

§3.5 Making decisions with Lenses

A decision is choosing which lens to apply; a good decision is one that changes the path to your destination. The framework's biggest gift is telling you which decisions you do not have to make yet.

 DECISION PROCEDURE
 1. ANCHOR    name the destination. No destination -> no decision.
 2. ON-PATH?  if the decision isn't in the destination's required
              sub-graph -> DEFER. (Most "decisions" die here.)
 3. LEVEL     find the decision's level/dimension -> pick the matching
              LENS. Decide architecture with the architecture lens,
              not the implementation lens.
 4. REAL?     do the options lead to DIFFERENT downstream sub-graphs?
              If not -> not a real decision. Pick either, move on.
 5. WEIGH     reversible -> decide fast, revise later. Irreversible ->
              apply MULTIPLE lenses (viewpoint/concern/time); where the
              lenses DISAGREE is the actual tradeoff to resolve.
 6. RECORD    node + provenance: destination, lenses used, choice, why.

Worked decision — "B1 classifier: single LLM call vs fine-tuned model?":

StepApplied
1 Anchordestination = ship B1 intake (v1)
2 On-path?yes — the classifier is in B1's closure
3 Level / lensL3-component + AI lens — not a strategy decision
4 Real?yes — fine-tune pulls in a whole data+training sub-graph; single-call doesn't. Different paths.
5 Weighreversible-ish (the intent{} contract is unchanged either way) → lean decide-fast. Lenses: cost (fine-tune expensive), time (single-call ships now), accuracy (eval lens: spike hit 17/20 ≥ threshold). Lenses agree → single-call for v1.
6 Recordprovenance: chose single-call; lenses [cost, time, accuracy, reversibility]; revisit if eval pass-rate drops below 90%.

Eliminates: off-path agonizing (step 2), false decisions (step 4), wrong-level thrash (step 3). Adds: effort calibrated to reversibility (step 5), multi-lens only for the genuinely hard ones (step 5), replayable rationale (step 6).

§3.6 The 6 HOW dimensions (operator-facing surface)

The 6 dimensions below are the operator-facing surface of §3.2's axes — they are the specific lenses an operator picks when working with Native today. Each row reads "what the operator is choosing" and "the values available."

DimensionThe question the operator answersExample choices
ApproachTop-down, bottom-up, or by analogy?Top-down: goal → factors → tasks · Bottom-up: data → patterns → conclusion · Analogy: this looks like prior X
MindsetOptimize · explore · repair · audit?Optimize: maximize known metric · Explore: surface options · Repair: fix broken state · Audit: verify-only
StructureTree of sub-tasks · sequential pipeline · parallel scan · iteration loop?Tree: T3 Charter decomposition · Pipeline: T1 multi-action · Scan: T2 iterative · Loop: refinement
DetailingCoarse first pass + sharpen · or fully detailed from start?Coarse-first: T0/T1 fast iterations · Detailed-first: T3 charter with locked requirements
DecompositionBreak into 3 / 7 / by-stakeholder / by-deliverable / by-risk?3-factor: forced parsimony · 7-factor: completeness · by-stakeholder: comms-heavy · by-risk: high-stakes
CascadingWhen a sub-task gets stuck, recurse on the sub-task?Sub-T3 spawns child T3 with its own Lens choices; depth bounded by complexity budget

              operator intent (B1) + task tier (T0-T3)

        ┌─────────────────────────────────────────┐
        │  LENS SELECTION                          │

Maps to §3.5 decision procedure — Anchor → Level → Real? → Weigh → Record.

§3.8 Lenses are everywhere — cross-section presence

Where Lenses show upWhat they mean there
§2.B B1 (Ask my way)Native parses operator's lens preferences from utterance (e.g., "quickly" = coarse-first detailing)
§2.B B3a (THINK)Brainstorm partnership uses Explore mindset; declares lens choice before deepening
§2.B B3b (DO)Auto-execute uses Optimize mindset by default; switches to Repair when output fails verification
§4 Native runtimeLens manifest in state envelope binds method routing (§9 D.11), agent scope, token budget
§4.5 Test-before-concludeVerification step is itself a lens choice (audit mindset · by-deliverable decomposition)
§6 Distribution / onboardingOperator's first-week lens preferences are surfaced + ratified during Charter setup
§7 ObservabilityLens accuracy KPI — operator drop rate per lens choice; drift signals on method-route accuracy

§3.9 Acceptance criteria (theory v1)

Forward-looking note: this section is THEORY work; productization is the next step. v1 ships the doctrine + the Lens primitive + the recommended axes; v2+ will lock the surface and refine based on dogfood signal.

  • Every Native turn carries an explicit lens manifest in state envelope (operator can request "show me your lenses").
  • Lens choices learned from B5.d learned patterns improve future selections (drift signal triggers re-learning).
  • Cascade depth bounded by complexity budget (Charter-set); depth-exhaustion escalates to operator (Authority gate).
  • Operator can override any lens choice; override learned + applied to future similar tasks.
